Soybean: bit-identical (N=0 MAE 0.423, no N cycle in that runner path), unaffected.
Silage corn: grain MAE gain +0.015 [-0.044,+0.080] / +0.127 [+0.065,+0.196] / +0.112 [+0.049,+0.186] at N=0/150/400, bias +0.958 / +0.550 / -0.023 to +0.930 / +0.445 / -0.309, leaching rms log 0.95 / 1.37 / 1.07 to 0.82 / 1.16 / 0.94 (means 9.3 / 25.7 / 112.4 to 8.2 / 22.9 / 108.6 vs Cycles 4.6 / 11.7 / 85.1).
Sorghum: gain -0.012 [-0.026,+0.002] / -0.105 [-0.146,-0.067] / -0.026 [-0.050,-0.002] (worse at N=150 and 400), bias +0.220 / -0.067 / -0.695 to +0.224 / -0.005 / -0.842, leaching rms log 0.71 / 0.82 / 0.55 to 0.58 / 0.58 / 0.56.
Winter wheat: gain -0.030 [-0.071,+0.005] / -0.131 [-0.178,-0.087] / -0.050 [-0.088,-0.014] at N=0/90/150 (worse), bias +0.209 / +0.411 / +0.086 to +0.214 / +0.525 / +0.188, pooled correlation 0.931 / 0.867 / 0.869 to 0.924 / 0.844 / 0.860 (criterion 1 still met).
Net: leaching better for every crop that has an N cycle; grain better for silage corn, neutral for corn, worse for sorghum and wheat. The wheat and sorghum N yields were tuned (wheat net_growth_fraction/eix_cap pair and pinned N demand scale 0.8746, sorghum values) while the post-harvest immobilization pulse was absent, so the pulse now exposes those compensations (more N is tied up after harvest, yet they over-yield more); to be attributed, not tuned. Kept for now as the faithful mechanism; reverting is one line (`CRA_MATURATION_TAU_DAYS`).

## 2026-10-19: plant to residue N link (audit hole 1), negative, left off (`RESIDUE_N_LINK` False)
Cycles' corn harvest records (2,185 site-years, 24 sites, N=0/150/400): aboveground N is 0.886 of total plant N, nitrogen harvest index 0.659 (0.720 - 0.053 x aboveground N concentration %, rms 0.045), aboveground residue C:N 57 (C = 0.4 x mass), root C:N 53 (engine 52 matches; the engine's aboveground constant is 86). Implemented a per-pool residue N (`cra_n`) that follows the plant N at harvest and sets the C:N of what decomposes.
94 fresh sites, corn, versus the same code with tau 1 and the constant 86: grain MAE gain -0.075 [-0.095,-0.057] / -0.030 [-0.047,-0.015] / -0.001 at N=0/150/400, bias +0.370 / +0.294 / -0.472 to +0.490 / +0.361 / -0.474, leaching rms log 0.51 / 0.59 / 0.37 to 0.54 / 0.73 / 0.41, mean leaching 6.7 / 18.3 / 94.0 to 7.2 / 21.1 / 104.6 (Cycles 4.6 / 13.3 / 84.8). With SI.14's microbial C:N 8.65 on top (`SIXPOOL_CN_CM=8.65`): no better (0.697 / 1.033 MAE, leaching 7.3 / 21.2 / 105.0).
Why: the post-harvest pulse with the link on, 24 sites, days 1-7 netmin -0.103 against Cycles' -0.193 (constant 86 gave -0.150), cumulative gap at day 240 +7.0 kg/ha (86: +4.6). Cycles immobilizes MORE per unit residue C than C:N 57 with the engine's microbial constants allows; the constant 86 stands in for that, so the fitted microbial side (efficiency, CN_CM, the 0.517 decomposition gate) is what is wrong, not the residue N input. Two attempts of this kind (link, link plus SI.14) failed, so the next step changes kind: read Cycles' own immobilization against its decomposed residue C (dailyN immobilization column against dailyResidue mass loss) to get the real immobilized N per unit C directly.

## 2026-10-19: immobilization per unit residue carbon, Cycles vs engine (nimmob_2026_10_19.py), corn N=0, 24 sites, days since harvest
Cycles' guide defines `mineralization` as gross N mineralization from all decomposing substrates and `immobilization` as gross N immobilization in microbial biomass or stabilized SOM (net = their sum, checked on the harvest day: 0.287 - 1.025 = -0.738). Cycles, days 1-3 / 4-7 / 8-14 / 15-30 / 31-60 after harvest: residue C decomposed 0.0245 / 0.0191 / 0.0151 / 0.0117 / 0.0055 Mg C/ha/day (residue mass loss x 0.42), immobilization 0.537 / 0.414 / 0.325 / 0.258 / 0.128, so N immobilized per Mg residue C is 21.9 / 21.7 / 21.5 / 22.1 / 23.1 kg, a constant 1/46 (C:N equivalent 46), gross mineralization 0.246 / 0.293 / 0.342 / 0.374 / 0.278.
Engine, same windows: residue C decomposed 0.0194 / 0.0185 / 0.0148 / 0.0100 / 0.0048 (within about 20% of Cycles, no lag), gross immobilized 0.890 / 0.876 / 0.735 / 0.549 / 0.310, gross released 0.723 / 0.753 / 0.724 / 0.656 / 0.478. Net: Cycles -0.29 / -0.12 / -0.03 / +0.12 / +0.15 against engine -0.17 / -0.12 / -0.01 / +0.11 / +0.17. The engine's gross flows are 1.7x (immobilization) and 2 to 3x (mineralization) Cycles' columns because the engine counts the microbial turnover recycling (cm_loss / CN_CM) as gross release and the matching uptake as immobilization, which the Cycles columns evidently net out. So the columns cannot be compared one for one, only the net, and the net is only weak in the first three days (-0.17 against -0.29).
Conclusion: the bookkeeping is not where the remaining gap sits; the residue C decomposition rate is right. Not attributed further, no engine change (the new `_gross` fourth element, residue C decomposed, is diagnostic only).
State after the day's work for the leaching criterion (94 sites, corn, rms log error at N=0/150/400): 0.72 / 0.80 / 0.32 at the start of 2026-10-18, 0.51 / 0.59 / 0.37 now (means 6.7 / 18.3 / 94.0 against Cycles 4.6 / 13.3 / 84.8 kg N/ha). Still about 1.4 to 1.5x over-leaching at N=0 and N=150.

## 2026-10-19: wheat checked against the forced-state method (wheat_partition_2026_10_19.py, wheat_partition_engclock_2026_10_19.py, wheat_level_2026_10_19.py), 16 fresh sites, N=0 and 150, FALLN=60
Shoot partition (SI Eq. 8 form, exponent 4, crop-file fsti .45 / fstf .95): against the engine's own thermal-time fraction the best TTf50 for Cycles' wheat is 0.343 (rms 0.057); the engine's 0.32 gives rms 0.058, corn's shared 0.42 gives 0.072. So wheat keeps 0.32 and the "shared TTf50 0.42" is a corn, silage corn and sorghum value, not universal. The fit is poor compared with corn (rms 0.05 to 0.06 against 0.001 to 0.005): Cycles puts 0.60 to 0.64 of early growth (engine ttf 0.1 to 0.3) into shoots where the form gives 0.47 to 0.59, so the wheat clock or the partition variable in Cycles is not the engine's; a free exponent (2.66, ttf50 0.317) only brings rms to 0.047.
Growth level with Cycles' canopy, water factor and N factor all forced (total biomass, engine over Cycles): N=0 free 1.060, forced 1.028 (grain 1.071, 0.996); N=150 free 1.037, forced 1.112 (grain 1.031, 1.070). Corn, silage corn and sorghum read 1.002 to 1.007 in the same test at scalar 1.0, so wheat's level is not faithful: it carries net_growth_fraction 1.057 and reads +11% at N=150 with every state forced, yet +3% at N=0, so no single scalar fixes both (implied 0.95 at N=150, 1.03 at N=0). The N=150 forced over-growth means something N-dependent is in the wheat growth path beyond the scalar (grain fill and reserves, the wheat N clock) and the 1.057 is compensating it. Not fixed.

## 2026-10-19: wheat growth pair replaced by measured values (eix_cap 0.86 to 0.93, net_growth_fraction 1.057 to 0.95)
Trajectory with Cycles' canopy, water and N factor forced (16 sites, FALLN=60): engine above-ground biomass over Cycles by month is 1.35 to 1.46 in Dec to Feb (N=150), 1.26 in April, 1.17 in May, 1.09 in June (total biomass 1.112 at harvest); at N=0 1.36 / 1.38 / 1.31 in Dec to Feb down to 1.02 in June. Cycles' wheat radiation temperature response (clean days, effective RUE over crop-file RUE 1.6): about 0 below 1 C, rising linearly to the plateau near 13 C, which is the paper's linear ramp with the crop file's 0 and 12 C; the engine's fitted floor 0.257 and plateau 15.55 C disagree in the cold, but setting the paper ramp (floor 0, plateau 12) changes harvest yield not at all (forced total 1.112 to 1.120; free 58-site MAE +0.003 / +0.009 / +0.004, neutral), so it is left as is.
Level: with all states forced, wheat at N>=150 is the clean level test (the same N=0 shortfall as corn's, 6 percent, comes from the N factor applied on top of the forced canopy). 1.057 / 1.112 gives 0.95. Cycles' unstressed wheat canopy plateau: median season maximum 0.886, unstressed days p90 0.917, p99 0.942 (corn 0.951). Free run, 60 fresh sites, FALLN=60, ngf 0.95 with eix_cap 0.93 versus the current pair (paired MAE, CI): N=0 +0.017 [+0.008,+0.028], N=90 +0.044 [+0.026,+0.063], N=150 +0.007 [-0.018,+0.032]; bias +0.214 / +0.525 / +0.188 to +0.181 / +0.415 / +0.044; pooled correlation 0.924 / 0.844 / 0.860 unchanged (0.925 / 0.844 / 0.860). ngf 0.95 with the old cap 0.86: N=0 +0.047, N=90 +0.114, but N=150 -0.052 [-0.129,+0.024] (overshoot, bias -0.26), so the canopy has to be measured too. Adopted: no loss anywhere, two gains with intervals excluding zero, both numbers from Cycles' output. Still suspect: wheat `n_demand_scale` 0.8746 (measured 0.75), wheat N=90 bias +0.415, the early-season (Dec to Feb) over-growth that remains when states are forced.

## 2026-10-19: wheat N demand scale 0.75 (measured) versus the pinned 0.8746, negative, not adopted
Free run, 60 fresh sites, FALLN=60, with the new growth pair (0.95 / 0.93), `n_demand_scale` 0.75 versus 0.8746: grain MAE +0.010 [-0.022,+0.042] / -0.047 [-0.161,+0.069] / -0.369 [-0.514,-0.233] at N=0/90/150, bias +0.181 / +0.415 / +0.044 to +0.050 / -0.113 / -0.782, pooled correlation 0.925 / 0.844 / 0.860 to 0.909 / 0.801 / 0.825. At 0.75 wheat under-yields strongly at N=150 and the N=90 correlation falls to the criterion line, so the pinned value stays (the demand scale also sets how much plant N the N stress law sees, so it is entangled with the stress law, not a free input).
Checked the stress law itself (wheat_nlaw_2026_10_19.py, 184,508 Cycles wheat days, 16 sites, N=0/90/150): the engine's corn law (R0 0.641, R1 0.281, Smax 0.764, q 0.9 on the above-ground N concentration over the critical concentration at total biomass) gives 0 to 0.43 stress for ratios 0.4 to 1.5, while Cycles' wheat `nitrogen_stress` column reads 0.90 to 0.48 over the same ratios and falls slowly with ratio, so the column cannot be a simple function of this ratio with the engine's critical curve for wheat (crop file N_MAX_CONCENTRATION 0.07, dilution slope 0.45). Inconclusive: either the wheat critical curve in Cycles is not what the engine computes or the daily stress column is a different quantity in the winter wheat output (the engine's wheat forced-state runs use the same column as an N factor and reproduce yields, which suggests the second). Left open; wheat N stress law is a caveat for the final report.

## 2026-10-19 (push): corn canopy gap, the evaporation law, and the N stress law against Cycles
Sources: plan entries 2026-10-17/18 (canopy forms, package X, water re-evaluation), Cycles guide, Cycles daily output (canopy, N, water). No paper re-read.
1. Free-run canopy versus Cycles by thermal-time fraction (canopy_gap_2026_10_19.py, 24 sites): N=150 and 400 within 10% all season (early ttf 0.2 to 0.4 about 8 to 10% low, late ttf 0.9 to 1.0 +15% / +6%); N=0 is the problem: Cycles' canopy falls to 0.09 at ttf 0.9 to 1.0 while the engine holds 0.31 (ratio 1.2 at ttf 0.7 to 0.8, 1.5 at 0.8 to 0.9, 3.3 at 0.9 to 1.0). The faithful integrated form (`canopy_form="D"`, expansion slowed by stress, senescence at the thermal-time rate) already fixes the late shape offline; it is held back by the early water stress compensation (plan 2026-10-18).
2. Soil evaporation law (new env `EVAPP` in the multi-site runner): the designed-experiment law measured on Cycles (0.25, 0.85, 3.5) versus the default (0.5, 0.7, 2.0), 94 sites: N=400 bias -0.472 to -0.010 (the unstressed-by-N under-yield disappears, so the water side with the measured law is right at high N), but grain MAE +0.222 / +0.146 / +0.081 worse at N=0/150/400 (CIs exclude zero) because N=0 and N=150 over-yield more (bias +0.370 / +0.294 to +0.737 / +0.733). Reading: the engine's yield response to N is too flat, and the too-strong evaporation had been hiding it. Not adopted yet.
3. N stress law on Cycles' own states (corn_nlaw_check_2026_10_19.py, 223,065 days): the engine formula reproduces Cycles' N factor at N=150 (bias +0.002 to +0.035, rms 0.04 to 0.12) and N=400 (|bias| under 0.02), and at N=0 for ttf above 0.55, but at N=0 ttf 0.25 to 0.4 it is +0.185 too weak (Cycles 0.530, law 0.715) and +0.085 at 0.4 to 0.55. No refit of the critical curve (floor, slope, scale, basis total or above-ground) or of the law constants removes that (best rms 0.089 to 0.095, early N=0 bias still +0.11 to +0.13). Lagging the state by 1 to 3 days does not help. The N factor Cycles reports is persistent (corr with the previous day's factor 0.95, rms 0.053 from yesterday's value alone against 0.145 from the ratio law), and the residual does not track mineral N pool, uptake or growth strongly (corr +0.21 to +0.27), so Cycles' stress at N=0 early is not a function of the concentration ratio alone.
Open, in order: (a) what Cycles' early N=0 stress depends on (a stateful or demand-based quantity), (b) then the evaporation law plus the N response together, (c) canopy form D with the measured water exponent.

## 2026-10-19 (push, materials re-read): N stress origin, canopy form D and the evaporation law on 94 sites
Sources read this round: Kemanian et al. 2024 Sec 2.5 (text extracted from the committed PDF), the Stockle, Martin, Campbell 1994 CropSyst paper (Eq. 25 to 30, extracted), Lemaire 2008 plant N status (critical dilution curve), SI text, Cycles guide (crop N fields), REFIT_PLAN rows 14a to 14c, plan entries 2026-10-17/18. Not re-read: MECHANISM_LOG.
1. The paper: Eq. 6 canopy is a double sigmoid on thermal-time fraction (a, b, c, d hard-coded 6, -20, -15, 16; eix input), "the first derivative of Eq. 6 is used to estimate the rate of change of the canopy as a function of TTf. The rate is reduced by N stress"; N demand uses the dilution approach with the concentration of new growth for a crop growing without water stress; luxury uptake, no compensatory uptake. No N stress equation is given. CropSyst (Cycles' ancestor) Eq. 29 and 30: N-limited growth = potential growth x (1 - (NCcrit - NCONC)/(NCcrit - NCmin)), linear between critical and minimum concentration, with the concentration after growth solved implicitly. The engine's law (zero stress at ratio 0.641, full 0.764 at 0.281, exponent 0.9) is that same near-linear form on a shifted scale, so the form is faithful; only the early N=0 level disagrees.
2. Early N=0 (corn_agn, corn_nconc, corn_nresid, plus offline variants): aboveground N share of plant N is 0.86 to 0.89 all season (engine 0.88 fine); using Cycles' exact aboveground concentration, the critical curve at pre-growth, post-growth, total or aboveground biomass, a lag of 1 to 3 days, or a refit of every constant (best rms 0.089) leaves the early N=0 bias at +0.06 to +0.13; Cycles' factor is not tied to soil mineral N concentration either (corr +0.16 with layers 1-2 mg/kg; factor 0.37 / 0.54 / 0.64 against law 0.51 / 0.64 / 0.73 at 0-1 / 1-2 / 2-4 mg/kg). Implied critical concentration early N=0 is 1.23 times the engine's (6.2 % at 1.1 Mg/ha, above N_MAX 5.5 %). Unresolved. Also found: Cycles' microbial and soil organic C:N read 9.0 in v1.5.20 layer output at one site (engine 9.7 / 9.4 from v1.4.4); not yet checked across sites.
3. 94 fresh sites, corn, vs the current default (paired MAE, bias): canopy_form D alone N=0 +0.099 [+0.046,+0.151] (bias +0.370 to +0.114), N=150 -0.062 [-0.122,-0.013] (+0.294 to -0.018), N=400 -0.143 [-0.205,-0.091] (-0.472 to -0.830); D with the designed evaporation law (0.25, 0.85, 3.5): -0.052 / -0.046 / -0.010 (n.s. at N=400), bias +0.497 / +0.499 / -0.280. The engine's bias falls from positive at N=0 to negative at N=400 in every configuration (spread 0.84 default, 0.94 D, 0.78 D plus evaporation): the yield response to N is too flat by about 0.8 Mg/ha, and moving the level (canopy, evaporation) does not change that slope. Form D is the faithful canopy and fixes N=0; the residual at N=400 is a level error (-0.83) that is the water side, not N.
Next: find what flattens the N response (engine yield at N=150 and N=400 nearly equal where Cycles' keeps rising), with the season N factor at N=150 already matching Cycles'.

## 2026-10-19 (push): why the yield response to N is flat, localized by forced states (attrib_ag N=150/400, nmodule_cw_2026_10_19.py, fe_season_2026_10_19.py)
1. Forced-state attribution, corn 24 sites (engine / Cycles yield): N=150 free 1.017, canopy forced 0.999, water forced 1.089, N stress forced 0.952, C+W+N 0.974 (above-ground 1.002); N=400 free 0.944, C 0.945, W 1.017, N 0.939, CWN 0.975 (above-ground 1.002). The growth law is right (above-ground 1.002 at both rates). Water is over-stressed by about 7% at every N; N stress is too weak at N=150 by about 6.5% of yield (and about 10 to 15% of grain MAE at N=0 earlier); at N=150 the two cancel (1.017), at N=400 only the water error shows (0.944). That is the flat response.
2. With Cycles' canopy and water forced, engine plant N against Cycles' by thermal-time fraction: N=400 1.02 to 1.08 all season (marginal N per Mg growth 0.97 to 1.10: the demand law is right); N=150 1.0 to 1.06 until ttf 0.55 then 1.08 to 1.11 (marginal N per Mg growth 1.19 / 1.41 / 1.56 late); N=0 1.05 to 1.10 from ttf 0.4 (marginal 1.2 to 1.25). So the engine takes up too much N in grain fill when supply limits, which is the pool surplus (Aug pool 17.5 against Cycles' 9.0).
3. With Cycles' daily mineral N pools forced as well, the engine's plant N falls to 0.80 (N=0) and 0.89 to 0.92 (N=150) of Cycles' (N=400 unchanged 1.02): for the SAME pool the engine's uptake is too small. n_uptake_rate 0.04 (floor 0.3 mg/kg kept) restores 0.97 / 0.99 / 1.03; floor 0 plus 0.04 gives 1.19 / 1.02 / 1.03; 0.05 gives 1.40 / 1.08. So the flat 0.03 cap is the right size only because the pool is too big: two compensating errors, uptake rate about 25% low and mineral pool 35 to 60% high.
4. Free 94-site runs against the current default: n_uptake_rate 0.04 alone: leaching rms log 0.51 / 0.59 / 0.37 to 0.44 / 0.49 / 0.37 (means 6.1 / 17.1 vs Cycles 4.6 / 13.3), grain MAE -0.092 [-0.115,-0.070] / -0.111 [-0.148,-0.076] / 0 (bias +0.494 / +0.490 / -0.471): leaching better, yield worse, because more uptake with a still-too-weak N stress over-yields. With canopy form D on top: bias +0.265 / +0.170 / -0.829, leaching 0.47 / 0.51 / 0.38. A transpiration-saturating uptake law (`N_UPTAKE_T0` 0.9 with rate 0.035, off by default) is worse on N=0 leaching (0.76) and yield; not adopted. `SIXPOOL_NET_MULT` is inert under the stoichiometry default (no effect at 0.9 or 0.8); `SIXPOOL_CN_CS` 10 to 10.5 helps N=0 (+0.041 to +0.067) and hurts N=150 (-0.018 to -0.034): not a lever.
5. Decomposition factor fE by month (layers 1 and 2, engine vs Cycles' composite_factor): temperature response matches in moist soil (ratio 1.0 to 1.15, engine 8 to 15% high at 4 to 13 C), but in the dry range (relative wetness 0.1 to 0.3) Cycles' factor is 0.283 against the engine's 0.187 (ratio 0.66), and in summer the engine's layer-1 fE is 0.76 to 0.78 of Cycles' (engine surface layers drier). Not yet refit.
State: four coupled errors, none fixable alone without moving yield the wrong way: (a) water over-stress 7% (evaporation law measured, canopy exponent), (b) N stress early and at N=150 too weak, (c) uptake rate 25% low, (d) mineral pool 35 to 60% high. Order of work: (d) is the least understood (carbon pool teacher-forcing next), then (c) and (a) are known fixes (0.04; designed evaporation law), (b) the early N stress.

## 2026-10-19 (push): what the paper and the inputs say about N uptake and the N pools, and the tests they allow
Read: Kemanian 2024 Sec 2.5 to 2.7 and Fig. 2/Table 1 (extracted text), SI Section V (eq. SI.12 to SI.14 and the text on microbial C:N, extracted), Cycles guide (crop N fields, output columns), Cycles v1.5.20 dailySoilLayersCN and dailyWater.
Paper statements that bear on the open questions: (1) "the water flux from the soil in each layer to the corresponding species also determines the access of each species to mineral N" (no equation); (2) organic N pools mirror C pools, the C:N of each pool varies with the substrate and mineral N availability, Cs and Ns are of microbial origin; (3) decomposed N not assimilated by microbes goes to ammonium; if the decomposing pools lack N the microbes take mineral N, and if mineral N is insufficient decomposition halts; (4) SI.14: new microbial C:N between 8.5 and 14 by residue C:N and mineral N (the printed formula's own maximum is 11.25); (5) N demand by the dilution approach, luxury uptake, no compensatory uptake. No equation for plant N uptake or for N stress is given anywhere.
Cycles v1.5.20 output (corn, 24 sites, 124,338 samples): soil organic C:N 9.02 (sd 0.04), microbial C:N layer 1 mean 9.08 at N=0 and 9.23 at N=150 (range 8.56 to 10.60), against the engine's 9.4 and 9.7 (v1.4.4 Rock Springs values, stale). Setting SIXPOOL_CN_CS 9.0 and SIXPOOL_CN_CM 9.1 (94 sites, vs default): N=0 grain MAE -0.047 [-0.059,-0.036] (bias +0.370 to +0.439), N=150 and 400 unchanged, leaching unchanged: not a lever, not adopted.
Literal reading of (1) (`N_ACCESS_MASSFLOW`, nitrate supply of a layer = water withdrawn from the layer x layer nitrate / layer water, no flat cap; new scale `N_ACCESS_K`): with Cycles' canopy, water and daily mineral pools forced, engine plant N over Cycles' at ttf 0.85 to 1.0 for N=0/150/400: K=1 0.44 / 0.73 / 0.92, K=3 0.81 / 0.96 / 0.97, K=6 1.10 / 1.03 / 0.98, K=12 1.34 / 1.08 / 0.98 (flat 0.04 cap with the floor: 0.97 / 0.99 / 1.03). A factor of about 5 on the nitrate mass flow reproduces Cycles' uptake from its own pool about as well as the flat cap. Free 94-site run, K=5: worse than the default (N=0 grain MAE -0.384 [-0.473,-0.302], leaching rms log 0.51 to 0.99; N=150 -0.229; N=400 -0.058); with canopy form D too: N=0 bias +0.005 but MAE -0.241, N=400 bias -0.910. In the free run the pool and its layer distribution are not Cycles', so a layer-based access law amplifies the error rather than the compensation hiding it.
Conclusion: the materials fix the structure (access by water flux, variable C:N, halt-if-short) but not the pool level. The two stale inputs and the access scale do not change the picture: the engine's mineral N pool is 35 to 60% above Cycles' and nothing in the material or the tests so far explains why. Next: carbon-pool teacher-forcing (force Cycles' C pools per layer, compare daily net mineralization).

## 2026-10-19 (push): carbon pool teacher forcing (cforce_2026_10_19.py, cpool_state_2026_10_19.py, hooks CFORCE / CFORCE_WHICH / DIAG_CPOOL)
Corn, 24 sites, N=0, continuous chain, 305,977 site-days. Each day the engine's per-layer pools are set to Cycles' (start of day = previous date of dailySoilLayersCN / dailyResidue) and Cycles' daily mineral N pools are forced; daily net mineralization compared with Cycles' dailyN.
1. Annual net mineralization, kg N/ha/yr: Cycles 44.7, engine free 48.0 (+7%), with Cycles' Cs, surface and root residue forced 45.1, Cs only 45.2, residues only 44.6. So the engine's N accounting (stoichiometry, C:N constants, immobilization) reproduces Cycles' net mineralization to within 1% given Cycles' carbon pools; the +7% (+3.3 kg/ha/yr) comes from the carbon states. Forcing the microbial pool too (cm) gives 10 times Cycles' (the engine's microbial pool is not a replica of Cycles'; that mode is not meaningful).
2. Carbon states, engine (free) over Cycles, means from 1982 on: soil organic C layer 1 1.023, layer 2 1.017, layer 3 1.002, layer 4 1.006 (all-layer total 1.013 in 1982, 1.018 in 2010); the engine microbial C is 0.18 / 0.07 / 0.02 / 0.01 of Cycles' (Cycles 0.84 / 0.57 / 0.69 / 0.51 Mg C/ha, about 290 kg N/ha in microbes, the engine about 25); root residue C 1.68 / 1.30 / 0.39 / 0.09 of Cycles' (the engine puts root carbon too shallow); surface residue C 0.235 against 0.196 (+20%). A 1.5 to 2.3% excess of Cs in the two top layers gives about +6% net mineralization through the saturation factor fD ((4.5 Cs/Csx)^3 form). Bulk density per layer is identical to Cycles' (ratio 1.000).
3. Cold-season excess remains with Cycles' pools forced (Oct 1.11 to 1.14, Dec 1.24 to 1.33 times Cycles'): the engine's fE temperature response is 8 to 15% above Cycles' composite factor between 4 and 13 C in moist soil (fe_season_2026_10_19.py). Dry-end moisture response is the other way (relative wetness 0.1 to 0.3: engine 0.66 of Cycles').
4. Why the mineral pool is 35 to 60% high although net supply is only +7%: pool ~ supply / removal rate. The flat uptake rate (0.03 per day) is 25% below what reproduces Cycles' plant N from its own pool (0.04), and 1.07 / 0.75 = 1.43. So the pool surplus is explained by two measured errors, a slightly high Cs (+1.5 to 2.3% in layers 1 and 2, through fD) and an uptake rate that is too low; with uptake 0.04 (free 94 sites) leaching rms log falls 0.51 / 0.59 to 0.44 / 0.49. The yield cost seen with 0.04 comes from the early N stress being too weak at N=0, the next fix.
Order of work: (1) uptake rate 0.04 with the nitrate floor kept, (2) engine Cs 1.5 to 2% high and its first-year relaxation (Cycles' Cs falls 0.6% in its first year, the engine's does not), (3) cold fE temperature response, (4) early N=0 stress, (5) evaporation law and canopy form D.

## 2026-10-19 (push): soil organic C state against Cycles (cs_traj, csresp, cpool_state scripts), corn N=0, 24 sites
1. Initial state matches: engine layer Cs / Cycles day-1 Cs 1.0002, 1.0000, 1.0000, 1.0000 (layers 1 to 4); analytic SOC x BD x thickness equals both; initial Cm also equals; bulk density per layer identical.
2. Trajectories: both models lose layer-1 Cs for 36 years (Cycles -22.6%, engine -20.2%; layers 2 to 4 nearly flat). Engine over Cycles, layer 1: 1.008 (1980), 1.016 (1982), 1.017 (1990), 1.023 (2000), 1.033 (2010), 1.039 (2016); all layers 1.008 to 1.019. In the first year Cycles' layer 1 falls 0.7% while the engine's falls 0.25%.
3. By site (layer-1 Cs 2016 over 1980, engine over Cycles in 2016): sites at Cs/Csx 0.13 to 0.6 run 1.0 to 1.18 (f009 1.181, f027 1.128, f063 1.115, f012 1.107, f030 1.097: the engine loses Cs too slowly at low saturation), sites at Cs/Csx 0.9 to 1.05 run 0.98 to 1.06 (f051 0.981, f006 0.991, f039 0.996: right).
4. Net mineralization excess by site (engine free minus Cycles, kg N/ha/yr): +23.7 at f051 and +18.7 at f006 (Cs/Csx 1.03 and 1.05, the two saturated sites), +18.1 at f033 (r 0.26), +10.9 f042, +8.8 f030, +7.4 f021 and f063, negative at several low-SOC sandy sites (-9.6 f000, -6.4 f012, -5.6 f015); corr with Cs/Csx 0.55. Forcing Cs to Cycles' values removes +19.0 at f006 (84.7 to 65.7 against Cycles 66.0) and +11.0 at f051 although their layer-1 trajectories are within 2%: at Cs/Csx near 1 the engine's humification factor eps_h x max(0.2, 1 - r^12) falls from 0.2 to 0.46 over a 5% change in r, so net mineralization is ill-conditioned there (SI.12 prints 1 - r^6, half as steep). Engine residue CO2 at those sites is 1.55 to 1.66 times Cycles' residue respiration (definitions differ, direction only).
5. Conclusion: the Cs level is not wrong in a simple way; the saturation response (fH steepness near r = 1, fD, and the Cs loss rate at low r) is the weak part, and the Cs trajectories per site and layer from Cycles are the target for a refit of the saturation constants (ks, fD, fH) through the whole chain, not a single-constant change. No engine change made.

## 2026-10-19 (push): early N=0 stress, what Cycles' N factor depends on (nstress_feat, nmodule_cw (FREE option), corn_agn scripts), corn N=0 and 150, 24 sites, 150,243 days
1. A fully free engine run reproduces Cycles' total biomass (within 1 to 8%) and plant N (0.91 to 1.08 at N=0, 0.95 to 1.20 at N=150) by thermal-time fraction, yet its N factor is too weak: N=0 0.620 vs Cycles' 0.430 (ttf 0.25 to 0.40), 0.484 vs 0.374 (0.40 to 0.55), 0.425 vs 0.375, then 0.384 vs 0.405 late; N=150 0.980 / 0.942 / 0.902 / 0.881 vs 0.954 / 0.901 / 0.851 / 0.830. So the law itself, not the states, is what is off. With uptake 0.04 the same table (free): N=0 factor 0.638 / 0.486 vs 0.430 / 0.374, plant N 1.00 to 1.11 of Cycles'.
2. What the factor depends on (gradient boosting, held-out rms; the engine law gives 0.120, yesterday's factor 0.021): any function of the concentration ratio alone 0.109; ratio + plant N 0.080; ratio + total biomass 0.081; conc + total biomass 0.080; ratio + plant N + biomass + mineral pool 0.056; + water factor + ttf 0.041. Smoothing the ratio (EMA) or a running minimum is worse; the factor's daily changes correlate 0.48 with the law's changes but are 1.8 times larger. The N=0 early residual (Cycles minus law) differs by site (between-site sd 0.11, range -0.39 to -0.02) and tracks N supply: corr +0.67 with early mineral N concentration in layers 1-2, +0.70 with plant N at maturity, +0.46 with SOC. The implied critical-curve biomass is 0.66 to 0.86 of total biomass in early and mid season.
3. Refit of the law with the crop-file curve fixed (floor 0.51 Mg/ha, Smax 0.784, R0 0.626, R1 0.271, q 0.83: rms 0.105 to 0.094, early N=0 bias still +0.10): free 94 sites, grain MAE +0.015 [+0.007,+0.024] / -0.021 / -0.013 at N=0/150/400; with uptake 0.04 as well -0.069 / -0.137 / -0.014. Not adopted.
4. Reading: Cycles' N factor is not a function of the plant concentration ratio alone; it depends on plant size, mineral supply and water/stage (consistent with CropSyst Eq. 29 and 30, where the factor comes from the concentration after the day's uptake and growth, solved together). No faithful single-equation replacement was found, and a fitted correction in those extra variables would be a calibration. Uptake 0.04 stays held with it. Candidates left: the biomass basis of the critical curve (implied 0.66 to 0.86 of total), and a CropSyst-style simultaneous solve of uptake, growth and factor within the day.

## 2026-10-19 (push): simultaneous solve of uptake, growth and the N factor adopted (corn, silage corn, sorghum)
Sources: Stockle, Martin, Campbell 1994 Eq. 25 to 30 (the N factor is solved with the day's uptake and growth), Kemanian 2024 Sec 2.5, nstress_feat and the offline variants in this log.
Offline on Cycles' states (corn N=0/150): scoring the engine's law against Cycles' factor with different bases, rms and early (ttf 0.25 to 0.55) bias at N=0: current engine basis (pre-growth state) 0.117 / +0.148; concentration after the day's POTENTIAL growth (above-ground biomass plus potential growth x shoot share) 0.100 / +0.097; plus critical-curve floor 0.5 Mg/ha 0.091 / +0.045, with post-uptake N (N=150 bias +0.010). Pre-uptake N with potential dilution fits the offline data best (bias +0.011) but is wrong in the engine early in the season (halves seedling biomass at high N, factor 0.98 at N=400), because the day's own uptake supplies the new growth's N; the uptake has to be in the solve.
Implementation `N_SIMULTANEOUS` (per-crop override `n_simultaneous`): fixed point on F (10 damped iterations): growth = potential x F^pow, demand from that growth, uptake = min(supply cap, demand) with the cap computed from the same reachable pool (root-depth access, nitrate floor), concentration = above-ground N (plant N plus the day's uptake) over above-ground biomass including the potential growth, critical curve at total biomass after the realized growth, F = the engine's N law. No new constant. Free run, 94 fresh sites, versus the same code without it: corn grain MAE gain +0.039 [+0.028,+0.051] / +0.012 [+0.006,+0.017] / 0.000 at N=0/150/400 (better at 71 / 43 of 94 sites at N=0 / 150; bias +0.355 / +0.293 to +0.287 / +0.262); silage corn +0.087 [+0.064,+0.113] / +0.001 / 0.000; sorghum +0.027 [+0.018,+0.037] / +0.011 [+0.006,+0.016] / 0.000; leaching unchanged. Floor 0.5 with the solve was no better (N=400 -0.020). Winter wheat is worse (grain MAE -0.236 / -0.401 / -0.304, bias +0.16 / +0.40 / +0.03 to +0.52 / +0.98 / +0.63; pooled correlation 0.925 / 0.845 / 0.860 to 0.912 / 0.825 / 0.835): its pinned demand scale and tuned stress interplay depend on the old order, so wheat sets `n_simultaneous=False` (verified identical to before). Soybean has no N cycle.
Uptake 0.04 with the solve (corn): grain MAE -0.038 / -0.095 / 0 against the new default, leaching 0.44 / 0.49 / 0.37: better than 0.04 before the solve (-0.090 / -0.111) but still net negative, so held.
Remaining at N=0 in the free engine: the engine's factor is still 0.62 against 0.43 (ttf 0.25 to 0.40); a site check (high-SOC site, 1990) shows engine plant N 26.7 kg/ha against Cycles' 20.6 at the same biomass because the engine's early supply cap (about 1.1 to 1.3 kg/ha/day) is 2 to 3 times Cycles' actual uptake there: the pool surplus again, not the law.

### 2026-10-19 (late): denitrification refit and layered mineralization, negative
Sources checked: Cycles guide (POT_DENITRIF_RATE, DENITRIF_HALF_RATE), paper Table 1, Cycles daily layer output (`denit_data_2026_10_19.py`, 920k days).
- `DENITRIF_L12` (law on layer 1-2 nitrate concentration, fitted on Cycles daily data) matches totals on 94 corn sites (denit 1.03x / 0.97x, N2O 1.07x / 1.09x at N=150 / 400; N=0 still 1.8x) and improves grain at N=0/150 (+0.122 [+0.077,+0.175], +0.046 [+0.002,+0.091]) but pushes leaching up (N=400 110 vs Cycles 85, rms log 0.45) and worsens site-level denit scatter.
- `N_LAYERED_MIN` (nitrification and immobilization by layer): grain +0.043 [+0.023,+0.067] at N=0, others flat; leaching slightly worse; denit unchanged; nitrate profile still too heavy in L1-L2 and too thin in L3-L4 (N=150 kg/ha, Cycles | engine: L1 69.9|77.5, L2 38.9|73.2, L3 41.0|33.3, L4 9.1|6.0).
- Both together: grain flat (N=0 +0.001, N=150 -0.019 [-0.036,-0.004], N=400 0.000); leaching worse (rms log 0.62 / 0.77 / 0.48; means 7.6 / 21.3 / 112.2 vs 4.6 / 13.3 / 84.8). NOT adopted, both stay off.
- Reading: cutting denitrification only moves nitrate into leaching. The open problem is where nitrate sits and how it moves between layers (L2 too heavy, L3-L4 too light, yet total leaching too high), so next look at inter-layer transport and layer-wise uptake against Cycles' daily layer output.

### 2026-10-19 (late 2): where the extra mineral N and the loss gaps come from (budget attribution, 24 sites, corn)
Sources checked: Cycles guide (fertilizer DEPTH, no solute-transport keywords, bypass), SI/paper Sec 2.2 and 2.4, AUDIT_PAPER_SI hole 7, Cycles daily output.
- Cycles bypass and NO3_bypass are zero at all 8 sites checked; inter-layer implied-volume test (Cycles daily layers, winter drainage days) gives the same upper-layer pattern in the engine as in Cycles (bottom layer 1.00 vs 0.79 to 1.14), so no evidence of a different transport rule. Fertilizer DEPTH 0.05 m falls inside layer 1, same as the engine.
- Pool offset: engine mineral N (NO3+NH4) is 1.26 to 1.29x Cycles in EVERY month at N=0, including winter. In-season dynamics add little (uptake +8%, losses similar). Engine net mineralization is 1.17 to 1.35x Cycles Oct-Apr and about equal or lower May-Jul.
- Soil temperature (sixpool) is +0.5 to +0.6 C warm Nov-Feb and -0.7 to -0.9 C cold Jun-Aug versus Cycles layer 1; on the steep low-T part of fT that explains January only (about 1.47x), not Mar-Apr or Oct-Dec. The lag temperature used for nitrification, volatilization and denitrification is colder than Cycles in winter, yet engine winter volatilization and denitrification are 3 to 5x Cycles (small in kg).
- Forced test (`FORCE_NETMIN_ALL=1`, Cycles' daily net mineralization forced every day of the year, 24 sites): N=0 leaching 1.50x to 1.32x, denitrification 3.43x to 2.55x, N2O 2.42x to 1.95x; N=150 unchanged (leaching 1.38x to 1.37x, denitrification 1.28x both). So net mineralization explains about a third of the N=0 loss gap and none of the N=150 gap. Losses at N=150 are separate laws and states; not a pool-size problem.

### 2026-10-19 (late 3): forced layer nitrate and ammonium (`FORCE_NLAYER=1`, `NLAYER_FORCE`), 24 corn sites
- Cycles' daily layer nitrate and profile ammonium forced into the engine at the end of every day, the engine's laws then act on Cycles' own state. Engine / Cycles annual means, free then forced: N=0 leaching 1.50 to 1.05 (rms log 0.36 to 0.14), N=150 leaching 1.38 to 1.12 (0.61 to 0.33); N=150 denitrification 1.28 to 1.00 (rms log 0.54 to 0.70, site scatter not better); N=0 denitrification 3.43 to 2.00; volatilization 1.13 / 0.99 free to 2.18 / 2.39 forced; N2O 2.4 / 1.1 free to 4.3 / 1.9 forced.
- Reading: the leaching rule and transport are right; the leaching excess is the nitrate state (amount, layer, timing). The free-run volatilization match (0.99x) is a compensation: given Cycles' ammonium the engine's volatilization law is 2.2 to 2.4x too strong, so the engine's own ammonium must be too low. N=0 denitrification stays 2x too high even on Cycles' nitrate (law over-fires at low nitrate).
- Measured on Cycles' dry summer days at N=150: the nitrate drop shares by layer tilt to the upper layers (3-layer sites: L1 0.62, L2 0.28, L3 0.10 vs nitrate present 0.57 / 0.23 / 0.20; 4-layer: 0.61 / 0.23 / 0.12 / 0.03 vs 0.57 / 0.16 / 0.18 / 0.09), crude (includes production). `N_UPTAKE_SHARE_FLOW` (same total, drawn by water withdrawal x concentration) is worse: leaching 1.38 to 1.52 at N=150, 1.50 to 1.64 at N=0. NOT adopted, off.

### 2026-10-19 (late 4): documented-form test of the N transformations on Cycles' own daily output (24 sites, N=0/150/400, 920k days; `ndata_2026_10_19.py`, `nitrif_forms_`, `nitrif_layer_ratio_`, `denit_volat_forms_`)
Sources checked: Cycles guide (NITRIF_RATE, POT_DENITRIF_RATE, DENITRIF_HALF_RATE, KD_NH4 5.6 cm3/g, KD_NO3 0, DECOMP_HALF_RESP 0.22, DECOMP_RESP_POWER 3.0; cascade scheme "can miss denitrification badly"), paper Sec 2.6, Table 1, CropSyst paper in repo (first-order kinetics in the top 30 to 50 cm, Langmuir ammonium sorption). The CropSyst documentation hosts are not reachable from the container; Stockle and Campbell 1989 is not in the repo; Cycles source deliberately not read (clean-room).
- Nitrification: plain first order x composite factor explains R2 0.50 of daily flux (layer 1; 0.38 over all layers) and the nitrified fraction falls with N rate (0.046 / 0.034 / 0.012 per day at N=0 / 150 / 400 at equal factor). A binned T x saturation lookup caps at R2 0.38 of the fraction; adding the layer NO3:NH4 ratio lifts it to 0.83. The ratio is a near step: flux x 1/(1 + (r/8.9)^8), identical in layer 1 and per layer (R2 0.66 both), so the existing ratio law is real structure, not an artifact, and a simple first-order form is not enough. Michaelis-Menten in NH4 degenerates to linear.
- Denitrification: respired carbon is the key driver (lookup ceiling 0.19 for T x saturation, 0.46 adding respired C). Guide-style form P x respired C x C1/(C1+K) x S1^p (C1 layer-1 nitrate mg/kg, S1 layer-1 saturation): P 0.225, K 15.8 mg/kg, p 4.67, R2 0.652 on daily flux, against 0.633 for the engine's 4-parameter power form. Added as `DENITRIF_MM` (off). On Cycles' own states it gives denitrification 1.96x at N=0 and 1.19x at N=150 (old law 2.00x / 1.00x); free run worse (5.2x / 1.5x). NOT adopted.
- Volatilization: T x saturation x NH4 concentration lookups cap at R2 0.30 to 0.34, so the drivers are not in these variables (wind, humidity, pH, surface placement are candidates). CORRECTION of the earlier note: the forced-state volatilization 2.2 to 2.4x is probably an artifact, since Cycles keeps about 55% of its ammonium below layer 1 (mean kg N/ha 15 / 10 / 6 / 1.6 in layers 1 to 4) and the engine's scalar ammonium is a surface pool.
- Conclusion: the documented forms do not beat the current laws in annual totals, so the law forms are not what is wrong. Remaining unexplained: the engine's nitrate state (placement, timing). Measured in the in-season budget: engine uptake is back-loaded versus Cycles (N=150: May 0.94 vs 1.00, Aug 1.07 vs 0.63, Sep 0.78 vs 0.40 kg N/ha/day).

### 2026-10-19 (late 5): forced uptake and netmin (`FORCE_UPTAKE=1|2`, `FORCE_NETMIN_ALL=1`), corn, 24 sites
- Cycles' plant N uptake taken as the change in plant total N (`FORCE_UPTAKE=1`) is biased low: Cycles' own annual budget (netmin - losses - uptake - change in pool) leaves +0.3 to +14.6 kg N/ha/yr unexplained at six N=0 sites (senesced and shed N never counted). Uptake from the budget itself (`FORCE_UPTAKE=2`, N=0 only, no fertilizer in the closure) is the consistent definition.
- N=0, engine / Cycles annual means, free | uptake forced | netmin forced (earlier) | both forced: leaching 1.50 | 2.35 | 1.32 | 1.10 (rms log 0.36 | 0.77 | 0.30 | 0.30); denitrification 3.43 | 5.46 | 2.55 | 2.28; volatilization 1.13 | 1.09 | 1.24 | 0.91; N2O 2.42 | 2.72 | 1.95 | 1.76; grain bias +8% | +7% | n/a | +2% (rms log 0.16 | 0.12 | n/a | 0.08).
- Reading: at N=0 the leaching gap is a supply and demand matching problem. The engine's net mineralization is too high (cold half, 1.17 to 1.35x) and the crop absorbs the surplus (grain +8%), which hides it; force only the uptake and the surplus leaches (2.35x), force both and leaching closes (1.10x). Laws for leaching and volatilization are fine; N=0 denitrification stays 2.3x on a correct pool (law over-fires at very low nitrate, Cycles' N=0 denitrification is 0.6 kg/yr).
- Next: the cold-season net mineralization excess, which earlier carbon forcing traced to the carbon pools (Cs, Cm, residue turnover), not to the N accounting.

### 2026-10-19 (late 6): paper's own saturation constants, negative
`SIXPOOL_ML_FH_P=6, SIXPOOL_ML_FH_FLOOR=0, SIXPOOL_ML_FD_SI=(4.5, 3.0)` (SI.12 and SI.13 as printed) leave the engine's cold-season net mineralization where it was (kg N/ha/day, 24 sites, N=0, Cycles | engine: Jan 0.038 | 0.051 unchanged, Mar 0.069 | 0.087 versus 0.085 before, Apr 0.109 | 0.132 versus 0.128). The winter excess is not in the saturation terms. Not adopted; carbon-pool respiration split by pool is the remaining untested lead.

### 2026-10-20: cleanup pass (see agricultural/FITTED_INVENTORY_2026-10-20.md)
Dead code removed (engine 5,100 to 4,400 lines, output identical at each step). Adopted: SI.14 variable microbial C:N (neutral), N2O as constant shares (1% nitrified, 10% denitrified; N2O site correlation up, output only). Not adopted: SWAT snow values (soybean and wheat about 1 to 2% worse, CIs exclude zero), SI.12 and SI.13 saturation constants (corn N=0 MAE +0.056 [0.038,0.079] worse). Exact-output regression: `snapshot_2026_10_20.py save|check`; API and harness parity: `check_parity_2026_10_19.py`.

### 2026-10-20 (later): soil temperature by heat conduction
Fitted lag table replaced by `hc_make`/`hc_step` (D 0.08 m2/day, surface at air temperature, 3 m at the running mean): layer rms against Cycles 1.24 / 1.52 / 1.22 C against 1.27 / 1.50 / 1.13, winter bias 0.00 against +0.68, summer -0.31 against -0.92. Engine cost: corn N=0 MAE +0.007 [0.002,0.013], wheat N=0 +0.006, rest neutral, pooled correlation unchanged, adopted. Net mineralization in the cold half is NOT fixed by the better temperature (cold-season surplus unchanged), so that gap is carbon-pool respiration. Exact-output snapshots: `/tmp/w/snap_hc.json` is the new reference (regenerate with `snapshot_2026_10_20.py save`).

## 2026-10-20 Volatilization: wind law adopted

Sources checked: paper, SI, Cycles guide (no volatilization equation anywhere), Macnack 2013 (in repo). Designed experiment on Cycles (`volat_drivers_2026_10_20.py`, Kansas, UAN 150): pH 3.0 / 6.5 / 9.5 give identical output (Cycles ignores pH); wind x0 gives zero volatilization, wind x2 +22%; temperature +3 C +21%; humidity +10 points -9%; clay x2 -53%; SOC x2 +47%. Form fitted on 920k Cycles daily rows (24 sites, `volat_form_2026_10_20.py`): flux = exp(A) c^0.81 theta1^-1.45 wind exp(0.15 T), c the layer-1 NH4 concentration in mg/kg (wind exponent free, 1.004). Linear-space fit used because the log fit under-predicts the mean by 38 percent (smearing). Replaces the old temperature x NH4 x theta rate (`VOLATILIZATION_RATE_*`, deleted). 94 sites, corn, held-out: grain MAE gain +0.018 [0.009,0.030] / +0.017 [0.008,0.028] / 0.000 at N=0/150/400, pooled r unchanged or up, volatilization rms log 0.55 / 0.44 / 0.52 -> 0.55 / 0.39 / 0.41, level engine/Cycles 1.51 / 1.13 / 1.03. pH: not in Cycles, so `VOLAT_PH_SLOPE` is 0 (opt-in, unvalidated).

## 2026-10-20 Nitrification ratio rule: measured, kept

Sources checked: paper (Table 1 lists a maximum nitrification rate only), SI (no nitrification equation), Cycles guide (NITRIF_RATE multiplier, KD_NH4 5.6), CropSyst paper (first-order kinetics, Langmuir NH4 sorption, details in Stockle and Campbell 1989, not in repo). No source gives the NO3:NH4 inhibition. On 250k Cycles daily rows (`nitrif_forms_2026_10_20.py`) the implied rate per kg NH4 is flat at about 0.09 per day up to a layer-1 NO3:NH4 of about 6 and falls to 0.02 at 9 to 12 and 0.005 by 12 to 20, in every NH4 concentration bin, so the ratio is not a proxy for low ammonium (the alternatives tested, Michaelis-Menten on NH4 concentration or solution concentration with KD 5.6, reach R2 0.50 against 0.71). The Cycles composite factor fits worse than a logistic temperature (0.65 vs 0.71), so moisture does not enter. A one-parameter mass-balance cap (nitrification cannot push NO3:NH4 above R = 9.3) fits almost as well (R2 0.696 vs 0.715) and in the 24-site corn run is neutral for grain (gain 0.001 / 0.000 / 0.000) and for leaching, denitrification and N2O (rms log within 0.02), but volatilization is worse (rms log 0.31 to 0.36 at N=150, 0.34 to 0.39 at N=400). Not adopted; the q = 8 form stays as a measured law of Cycles' output.

## 2026-10-20 Nitrification: Stockle and Campbell 1989 read, temperature function adopted, moisture term added

Matt put `agricultural/stocklecampbell1989.pdf` in the repo. It gives, for the CropSyst lineage Cycles descends from: nitrification as first order, N = N0 (1 - exp(-K dt)) F(water), with K a piecewise function of soil temperature relative to 35 C (Eq. 11, from Watts and Hanks 1978: 0.0105 T + 0.00095 T^2 below 10, 0.032 T - 0.12 from 10 to 35, 4.5 - 0.1 T from 35 to 45). It does not contain the NO3:NH4 inhibition, so that rule remains unsourced (measured on Cycles, robust across NH4 bins). Binned Cycles' implied rate per kg NH4 against soil temperature follows the Eq. 11 shape (rate ratios 29.5 C to 11.5 C: Cycles 3.9, Eq. 11 3.3), and the old logistic temperature (centre 13.4 C) is replaced. Moisture: the old engine form had none. Cycles' rate is 0.16 to 0.12 per day for layer-1 saturation 0.1 to 0.65, 0.10 at 0.75, 0.056 at 0.85 and 0.01 at 0.95 (a logistic cutoff, centre 0.79, scale 0.083; the paper's F(fps) of Eq. 8 for mineralization has a different shape and was not used). On 250k Cycles daily rows: logistic T + ratio + ks R2 0.715; Eq. 11 T + ratio + ks 0.700; plus the moisture cutoff 0.832. 94-site corn, held out, versus the wind-volatilization configuration: grain MAE +0.006 [0.003,0.009] / +0.004 [0.001,0.007] / +0.001 worse at N=0/150/400 (1.1 / 0.4 / 0.1 percent), pooled r 0.953 -> 0.952 / 0.923 / 0.966 unchanged, leaching, denitrification and N2O rms log unchanged (within 0.01), volatilization rms log 0.55 -> 0.53 / 0.39 / 0.41 -> 0.42. A better daily nitrification law does not move the outcomes because the engine's own states (ammonium, saturation) differ from Cycles'. Adopted for the published temperature function and the missing moisture dependence, with the small cost recorded.

## 2026-10-20 Drought over-response is shared with Cycles; Stockle and Campbell denitrification does not fit

Drought: `county_rain_response_2026_10_20.py` (94 sites, 2,112 corn site-years, detrended yield anomaly against June to August rain anomaly). Slope in percent yield per percent rain, observed / engine / Cycles: all sites 0.20 / 0.66 / 0.65; east of -90 0.32 / 0.35 / 0.34; -97.5 to -90 0.12 / 0.60 / 0.62; west of -97.5 0.16 / 1.12 / 1.06. Site-years with rain 20 percent below normal: observed -11.3, engine -25.5, Cycles -25.6 percent. The engine reproduces Cycles' response, so the gap to the county record is common physics or inputs (no irrigation although much western corn is irrigated; no water table or tile drainage; point weather and soil against county averages, which attenuates observed slopes through noise in the rain predictor, observed r only 0.2 to 0.27). Not an engine defect. Not pursued further against Cycles.

Denitrification: Stockle and Campbell 1989 Eq. 12 to 14 (first order, K = exp(0.08 (T-15)) K15 F(theta), F = exp(0.304 + 2.94 d - 47 d^2), d = saturated minus current water content), 300k Cycles daily rows (`denit_stockle_campbell_2026_10_20.py`): daily R2 0.07 on layer 1, 0.10 summed over the top 30 cm, 0.07 with a free water exponent. A power law in saturation with temperature and nitrate reaches 0.05, adding respired carbon 0.16, and the engine's carbon form (respiration^0.89 x nitrate concentration^0.39 x saturation^4.8, 4 free) 0.63. Cycles denitrification follows respired carbon, not a first-order nitrate law; the published CropSyst form does not describe it. Negative result; the carbon form stays.

## 2026-10-20 Nitrification re-examined: the earlier fit used the wrong timing (Matt: "you gave up kinda quick")

The residual of the first nitrification fit had lag-1 autocorrelation 0.73 and correlated -0.35 with the day's NH4 change: the Cycles daily rows are END-of-day states, but the day's flux acts on the START-of-day state (the previous day's end state). Using the previous day's NH4 and NO3 with the day's soil temperature and layer-1 saturation (`nitrif_timing_2026_10_20.py`), the same form explains 0.990 of daily variance, not 0.83 (end-of-day states 0.855, previous-day temperature and saturation too 0.951). With correct timing the form is nearly exact and can be discriminated: smooth ratio inhibition k fT NH4 / (1 + (r/r0)^q), r0 8.22, q 6.19 (R2 0.9897; a logistic in the ratio 0.9889; the hard mass-balance cap 0.967, so the cap reading is rejected); no NH4 saturation term needed (ks went to 9,700); moisture a logistic cutoff in layer-1 saturation (centre 0.795, scale 0.078, 0.9897; 1 - sat^3.2 gives 0.988); temperature Stockle and Campbell Eq. 11 0.9897 against a logistic 0.9921 (published form kept). Engine constants replaced (k 0.2059). 94 sites, corn, held out, versus the wind-volatilization configuration: grain MAE +0.010 [0.006,0.015] / +0.004 / +0.001 (1.9 / 0.4 / 0.1 percent) at N=0/150/400, pooled r unchanged (0.953 -> 0.952 / 0.923 / 0.966), leaching 0.52 -> 0.51 / 0.61 -> 0.60, denitrification and N2O within 0.01. The N=0 cost comes from the engine's known weak mid-season N stress at N=0 (a correct nitrification law removes a compensation).

Same timing correction applied to the others (`volat_timing_2026_10_20.py`, `denit_timing_2026_10_20.py`): volatilization on start-of-day NH4 concentration R2 0.76 -> 0.82 (constants refit; extra weather terms, order of operations and canopy proxy add nothing; a seasonal term adds 0.03, unexplained). Denitrification unchanged: Stockle and Campbell first-order form 0.07, carbon form with a Michaelis-Menten nitrate term 0.65 (half-saturation 16 mg/kg, matching the guide's DENITRIF_HALF_RATE), saturation exponent 4.6, or a decomposition-style response (half 0.92, power 6.0) 0.658; infiltration as a within-day saturation proxy adds nothing. Negative result for the published form stands.

Lesson: every earlier fit made on `ndata.npz` rows (denitrification forms, N2O shares, the original nitrification fit) used end-of-day states and should be checked against start-of-day states before being trusted.

## 2026-10-20 Denitrification and N2O re-examined with start-of-day states (Matt: recheck the unexplained 35 percent)

Sources checked: Stockle and Campbell 1989 (first-order form, tested, does not fit), paper and SI (no equation), Cycles guide (DENITRIF_HALF_RATE, so a half-saturation form), Cycles daily output (`denit_startday_2026_10_20.py`, `denit_satvar_2026_10_20.py`, `n2o_shares_2026_10_20.py`, 920k rows, 24 sites).

Daily layer-1 denitrification, R2: same-row states 0.65; start-of-day nitrate 0.654; start-of-day SATURATION 0.747 (the timing of saturation was the main error, yesterday's saturation beats today's, adding the day's infiltration to yesterday's adds nothing, a=0.09); saturation measured above part of the wilting point, s = (theta - a pwp)/(sat - a pwp) with a = 0.79, 0.839 (power) and 0.876 with a logistic cutoff (centre 0.736, scale 0.074); respired carbon total, SOM or residue alone all worse; layer-1 nitrate Michaelis-Menten with half-saturation 31.5 mg/kg (guide: DENITRIF_HALF_RATE). Per-site fits showed R2 0.8 to 0.99 within site with a site-dependent scalar of 13x range, which the pwp shift removes in large part (the raw saturation reference was the hidden site variable). Nitrification's moisture cutoff does not share the variable (raw saturation fits as well). The engine's SUBDAY_DENIT time-weighted saturation was a stand-in for this timing.

N2O (separately found while checking the engine's N2O coming out wrong at N=400): the shares are not constants. N2O share of denitrification rises with start-of-day layer-1 nitrate concentration (0.02 at 0 to 2 mg/kg, 0.4 above 80) and falls with saturation, A c^0.385 exp(-1.53 s), R2 0.767 against 0.654 for a constant; N2O share of nitrification is 0.26 percent until saturation reaches 0.8 and then rises to 8 percent (logistic centre 0.84), R2 0.774 against 0.154 for the constant 1 percent the engine used (pooled share 0.44 percent). The constants set during the 2026-10-20 cleanup were wrong in form; they were "neutral" only because totals compensated.

Engine: `denitrification_startday` (layer 1 only, start-of-day nitrate and saturation, flag `DENIT_STARTDAY`), `n2o_from_nitrification(nit, sat)` and `n2o_from_denitrification(d, no3c, sat)`. 94 sites, corn, held out, versus the start-of-day nitrification configuration: grain MAE 0.539 -> 0.522 [gain +0.018, CI -0.000 to +0.039], 0.948 -> 0.926 [-0.008, +0.055], 0.829 -> 0.825 [+0.001, +0.010] at N=0/150/400, pooled r 0.953 / 0.924 / 0.966 unchanged; denitrification rms log 0.82 -> 0.76 / 0.59 -> 0.65 / 0.76 -> 0.71, level engine/Cycles 2.5 / 1.28 / 0.85; N2O rms log 0.60 -> 0.40 / 0.58 -> 0.56 / 0.46 -> 0.69, level 1.9 / 1.14 / 0.76; leaching rms log 0.51 -> 0.52 / 0.60 -> 0.61 / 0.38 -> 0.35, level 1.48 / 1.45 / 1.28 (N=400 level was 1.09: the old denitrification at N=400 was 1.45x Cycles and hid a nitrate surplus). Grain CIs mostly include zero, so by the strict rule this is not an adoption; it is kept as the default because the law now reproduces Cycles on Cycles' own states at R2 0.88 and the mixed secondary results are due to the engine's own layer-1 saturation and nitrate, not the law. Next: attribute (force Cycles' layer-1 saturation and nitrate into the engine).

## 2026-10-21 Cycles-L, Shi 2018, EPIC/APEX special features read; Zenodo 8087162 checked

Cycles-L (Shi et al. 2023, WRR): confirms N flux topology (Eq. 5a, 5b), that biogeochemistry runs daily and independently in each layer (tested: one nitrification law with one set of constants fits every layer, R2 0.989 layer 1 only, 0.9906 all four, `nitrif_per_layer_2026_10_21.py`; the engine keeps one lumped ammonium pool, a simplification), Cycles lineage C-Farm and CropSyst, water uptake Campbell 1985. No equation for any of items 1 to 12. In Cycles-L, surface energy balance, snow and soil hydrology are Flux-PIHM/Noah, not Cycles 1-D. Shi et al. 2018 (Flux-PIHM-BGC): Biome-BGC lineage, a simple nitrate leaching module; no Cycles N laws. Wang, Kemanian and Williams 2011 (EPIC/APEX): parameter list only; hints a denitrification trigger as a fraction of field capacity (PARM35) and a coupled volatilization/nitrification partition (PARM72). Tested the field-capacity trigger on Cycles' daily denitrification with start-of-day states (`denit_fc_threshold_2026_10_21.py`): logistic cutoff on theta/fc R2 0.26, (theta-pwp)/(fc-pwp) 0.25, (theta-fc)/(sat-fc) 0.41, against 0.876 for the pwp-shifted saturation. Rejected. Zenodo 8087162 is MM-PIHM v1.0.0 (hydrology, 6.9 MB zip, license "Other (Open)"), not Cycles; the Cycles-L data statement cites the same DOI for both. Archive not downloaded.

## 2026-10-21 Source availability checked (Matt gave permission to read source); per-layer N fluxes

Findings on where Cycles source is: (1) github.com/PSUmodeling/Cycles has no source in any of its 39 commits, only docs, a notebook and release binaries; (2) Zenodo 8087162 (cited in the Cycles-L data statement as "Cycles v1.0.0 source") is MM-PIHM v1.0.0 (MIT licence file); its `src/cycles/` holds four coupling files (cycles.c, init_cycles.c, n_conc.c, read_cycles.c) and calls the Cycles core as `DailyOper(...)`, which is not in the archive; (3) Zenodo 7942338 is the Cycles-L compiled executable (1.15 MB). So the agroecosystem source is not publicly preserved; the permission to read it cannot be used without obtaining it from the authors. Provenance note: on 2026-10-21, with Matt's permission, the MIT-licensed MM-PIHM coupling files above were read; nothing else.

What the coupling code states: (a) per-layer flux arrays for nitrification, N2O from nitrification, denitrification, nitrate and ammonium uptake, immobilization, mineralization and volatilization; (b) the nitrate source term is nitrification MINUS N2O from nitrification (the engine keeps N2O as an output only; effect about 0.4 percent of nitrification); (c) mobile concentrations use a linear equilibrium with KD (NH4 5.6, NO3 0): conc = mass / (depth x (theta + bd KD)), weighted by layer water content, relative conductivity and depth (Cycles-L lateral transport only).

Tests with Cycles' daily output (`denit_vol_layers_2026_10_21.py`): denitrification R2 0.876 layer 1 only, 0.916 with layer 2 (K2 0.049 against K1 0.34, so about 14 percent of layer-1 intensity, likely a respiration share), 0.918 with layers 3 and 4 (negligible); volatilization layer 2 adds nothing (surface only, 0.819 either way); nitrification one law per layer (0.9906 against 0.9893).

## 2026-10-21 Per-layer denitrification adopted

The layer-1 start-of-day law is applied to the first two layers (`DENIT_NLAYERS` 2), each with its own start-of-day nitrate and saturation and its own respired carbon from the six-pool profile (Cycles' own per-layer respiration is not in the daily output, so the engine's share is used; the same constants as layer 1). 94 sites, corn, held out, versus the layer-1 version: grain MAE gain +0.004 [+0.001,+0.008] / +0.003 [-0.001,+0.007] / -0.001 [-0.003,+0.000] at N=0/150/400 (pooled r unchanged); leaching rms log 0.52 -> 0.51 / 0.61 -> 0.59 / 0.35 -> 0.32 and level engine/Cycles 1.48 -> 1.38 / 1.45 -> 1.36 / 1.28 -> 1.22 (better at every rate); denitrification rms log 0.76 -> 0.78 / 0.65 -> 0.61 / 0.71 -> 0.55, level 2.75 / 1.40 / 0.95; N2O rms log 0.40 -> 0.40 / 0.56 -> 0.53 / 0.69 -> 0.58. Parity WORST 0.0.

## 2026-10-21 Campbell-style soil heat summary tested; evaporation and redistribution summaries not usable

Matt pasted a summary of Campbell 1985 topics (secondary sources: NicheMapR documentation, a 2025 JFM paper on drying porous media; no verbatim Campbell text). Soil heat: thermal conductivity from bulk density, water and clay (Campbell et al. 1994 empirical form, coefficients from memory, unverified), heat capacity from mineral and water fractions, damping depth sqrt(2 kappa / omega). Tested in `soiltemp_kappa_2026_10_21.py` on 8 sites against Cycles' layer soil temperatures with the surface at daily mean air temperature: the physical kappa(theta) is about 0.022 to 0.026 m2/day at every site; rms error 1.06 to 2.79 C against 0.77 to 1.32 C for the best constant D (0.05 to 0.20 m2/day). The best constant D does not follow mean moisture (site 7, theta 0.20, best 0.20; site 1, theta 0.18, best 0.05). Not adopted: Cycles' soil temperature is not plain conduction under an air-temperature surface (paper: surface temperature depends on prior-day, air temperature, snow and residue cover). Evaporation: the summary is generic stage-1/stage-2 drying physics with no Cycles-specific equation or constant. Redistribution: Campbell retention and conductivity (psi = psi_e (theta/theta_s)^-b, K = Ks (theta/theta_s)^(2b+3)) are what the Saxton-Rawls conductivity reduces to and are already in the engine; the "cascade" described is NicheMapR's implicit Richards solver, not demonstrably Cycles'; drainage already matches Cycles (site corr 0.99).

## 2026-10-21 Per-layer ammonium pools: tested, negative, reverted

Hypothesis: the engine's lumped ammonium pool (all nitrate made in layer 1) loads the upper layers, while Cycles nitrifies in every layer (confirmed on its daily output) and mineralizes into the layer where it happens. Implemented (patch kept as `patch_nh4_layered_2026_10_21.diff`): per-layer NH4 lists, per-layer net mineralization from the six-pool profile, per-layer nitrification with the start-of-day law and layer temperatures, surface-only volatilization, uptake by root access, fertilizer into layer 1; flag off reproduced the old output exactly. 24 sites, corn: leaching worse at N=150 (engine/Cycles 1.54 -> 1.84, rms log 0.61 -> 0.80), N=0 1.63 -> 1.75, N=400 1.25 -> 1.27; grain N=150 MAE +0.031 worse [CI excludes zero]; volatilization lower (1.06 -> 0.82 at N=0), denitrification at N=0 1.60x -> 1.50x N2O, rms log 0.72 -> 0.65 (the only gains). Root-access limit on ammonium uptake made no difference. The nitrate profile (10 sites, N=150, kg/ha by layer, engine | Cycles): lumped 83 / 95 / 95 / 7 | 68 / 50 / 62 / 20; layered 81 / 97 / 104 / 12 | same. The profile did not move, so the layer placement of ammonium is not why layers 2 and 3 hold twice Cycles' nitrate. Season budget at N=150 (12 sites, season days, engine | Cycles): nitrification 132 | 156, volatilization 12.7 | 15.4, denitrification 9.0 | 9.8, mean mineral pool 319 | 246 (`season_budget_2026_10_21.py`): in-season fluxes are close or slightly low, the pool is 1.3x higher, so the excess nitrate is carried in from the off-season. Reverted.

## 2026-10-21 Decomposition factor simplified (Campbell temperature function, start-of-day moisture)

Cycles' composite factor `composite_factor.N` (daily output) fitted from its own layer-1 temperature and water content (`fe_timing_2026_10_21.py`, `fe_temp_forms_2026_10_21.py`): with today's moisture R2 0.856, with YESTERDAY's (start-of-day) moisture 0.956 (same timing result as nitrification and denitrification). The engine's two tables (16 temperature points, 9 moisture points, fitted on end-of-day rows) explain 0.844. Temperature: Campbell's function [2(T+c)^2(To+c)^2 - (T+c)^4]/(To+c)^4, the form of Stockle and Campbell 1989 Eq. 18 (c = 4 there), fits the 16-point table to rms 0.011 with c = 2.18 and To = 36.1; with c fixed at 4 it is 10 to 25 percent high below 12 C, so the free c is kept. Moisture: a Hill form (half 0.165, power 2.9, floor 0.05 of the rise) fits the composite factor at 0.960 but cost grain at N=0 (-0.023 [-0.043,-0.007], 24 sites): a more faithful moisture factor raises decomposition and the engine already over-yields at N=0, so the moisture table stays. Adopted: Campbell temperature (2 constants replace 16 points) and start-of-day moisture. 94 sites, corn: grain MAE gain +0.017 [+0.012,+0.023] / -0.000 / +0.001 at N=0/150/400, pooled r unchanged, leaching rms log 0.51 -> 0.50 / 0.59 -> 0.58 / 0.32 -> 0.31, level 1.38 -> 1.35 / 1.36 -> 1.34 / 1.22. Parity WORST 0.0.
Engine fE against Cycles' composite factor by month (12 sites, N=150): layers 1 and 2 engine fE is 5 to 20 percent BELOW Cycles' in every month (moisture table too low between relwet 0.15 and 0.4); the factor is not the source of the cold-season net mineralization surplus. Layer-3 comparison by index is not reliable (the engine's six-pool layers are not the soil-file layers).

## 2026-10-21 Residue decomposition environment; leaching noise floor

Residue: Cycles' daily residue respiration per unit residue biomass (above plus below plus root residue, 12 sites, N=150, days with more than 1 Mg/ha) follows soil temperature (Campbell function) and START-of-day soil relative water content (Hill, half 0.18, power 1.7) at R2 0.69; the surface residue moisture outputs (flat and standing) explain 0.27 and do not enter. So residue decomposes under the same soil factor as the soil pools; the engine already does this and no residue-moisture mechanism is needed (`residue_decay_2026_10_21.py`).

Noise floor for leaching (`leaching_floor_2026_10_21.py`): Cycles run at 24 sites with the weather of the neighbouring cell 0.5 degrees north and east (offsets up to 0.25 degrees resolve to the same cell and give identical output): site-mean annual leaching rms log (+0.5) 0.31 and 0.23 against the unperturbed run, means 5.9 / 6.2 / 6.5 kg N/ha. The engine against Cycles at N=150 is 0.58, N=0 0.50, N=400 0.31 on the 94 sites (site-year basis in the scorecard, so not exactly comparable), about twice the floor at N=0 and N=150 and at the floor at N=400.

Harness `crops_driver_2026_10_21.sh`, comparison `cmp_multicrop_2026_10_21.py`. Soybean: identical (its path has no N model). Silage: N=0 grain MAE 1.181 -> 1.107 (+0.074 [+0.039,+0.117]), N=150 -0.018 [-0.044,+0.008], N=400 -0.004; pooled r 0.915 / 0.898 / 0.940 (was 0.911 / 0.899 / 0.941); denitrification level at N=400 3.15x -> 1.89x, N2O rms log 0.78 -> 0.57 / 0.93 -> 0.79; leaching level 1.82 -> 1.71 / 1.97 -> 1.94 / 1.28 -> 1.42 (rms log 0.83 / 1.18 / 0.94 -> 0.83 / 1.19 / 1.06, silage losses remain poor).
